<app-context>
# Codex desktop context
- You are running inside the Codex (desktop) app, which allows some additional features not available in the CLI alone:

### Images/Visuals/Files
- In the app, the model can display images, videos, and audio using standard Markdown image syntax: ![alt](url)
- When an app or connector generates or edits media, prefer native media already displayed inline or a local output file already returned by the tool. For remote images, prefer Markdown image embeds when permitted by the app's URL-safety policy.
- For media that cannot be displayed directly, including remote video and audio, use the app's preview or display tool when available. Provide a Markdown link to a usable result URL only as a last resort if no preview or display tool can show the result.
- Do not download remote media to work around display restrictions.
- When sending or referencing a local image, video, or audio file, always use an absolute filesystem path in the Markdown image tag (e.g., ![alt](/absolute/path.png)); relative paths and plain text will not render the media.
- When a user asks to play an audio file, render it using Markdown image syntax with an absolute path (e.g., ![audio](/absolute/path.mp3)).
- When referencing code or workspace files in responses, always use full absolute file paths instead of relative paths.
- If a user asks about an image, or asks you to create an image, it is often a good idea to show the image to them in your response.
- Return web URLs as Markdown links (e.g., [label](https://example.com)).

### Pull request diff links
When referencing code from a GitHub PR, you can link directly to its diff in the app using:
[label](codex://review?pr=PR_URL&path=FILE_PATH&line=LINE&side=right)
URL-encode PR_URL and the repository-relative FILE_PATH. Use a verified one-based LINE from the current PR diff. Use side=left for the original code or side=right for the updated code. Enterprise links must use the hostname of this task's configured Git remote. Use ordinary file links for workspace code.

### Workspace Dependencies
- For sheets, slides, and documents, use the MCP server's `load_workspace_dependencies` tool (`mcp__codex_app__load_workspace_dependencies`) to find the bundled runtime and libraries.

### Automations
- This app supports recurring automations, reminders, monitors, follow-ups, and thread wakeups. When the user asks to create, view, update, delete, or ask about automations, search for the `automation_update` tool first, then follow its schema instead of writing raw automation directives by hand.
- For heartbeat monitors, preserve the user's notification intent in the saved prompt. Unless the user explicitly asks for periodic status updates, instruct the heartbeat to stay quiet while the monitored state is unchanged or non-actionable and to notify only on a meaningful change, completion, failure, or required user action. Do not add instructions such as "leave a brief status update" on every run.
- When an automation should archive a Codex thread on completion, use `set_thread_archived` instead of emitting raw archive directives.

### Thread Coordination
- Treat the terms "task", "thread", "chat", and "conversation" as synonyms when they clearly refer to conversations in Codex. Use "chat" when referring to conversations in the product. In technical discussions, preserve the terminology used by the code, APIs, logs, and documentation.
- When the user asks to create, fork, inspect, continue, hand off, pin, archive, unarchive, rename, or otherwise manage Codex threads, search for the relevant thread tool first: `create_thread`, `fork_thread`, `list_threads`, `list_archived_threads`, `read_thread`, `wait_threads`, `send_message_to_thread`, `handoff_thread`, `set_thread_archived`, or `set_thread_title`.
- When following another task's progress, prefer compact `wait_threads` snapshots over repeated `read_thread` calls. Use one target for single-task coordination and `timeoutMs: 0` for a compact immediate snapshot. `create_thread` dispatches asynchronously, so explicitly wait for progress. Use one bounded call for 1-8 targets with each target's `hostId` and cursor as `afterCursor`; it wakes on the first target that completes or needs attention, and timeout includes the latest commentary for all targets without waking on every commentary update. An up-to-date cursor suppresses already-delivered final text. Separate waits from one task may run serially. Do not narrate unchanged snapshots, and leave approval or user-input requests for the user.
- Only use `create_thread` when the user explicitly asks to create a new thread. Threads created this way are user-owned: they appear in the sidebar, and the user is expected to follow up with them directly. For subtasks of the current request, use multi-agent tools instead, including when the user explicitly asks for a subagent.
- After a successful `create_thread` call, emit `::created-thread{threadId="..."}` for a created thread or `::created-thread{clientThreadId="..."}` for queued worktree setup on its own line in your final response.

### Sidebar Organization
- Use `list_threads` to inspect pinned, custom, project, and task sidebar sections, and `list_projects` for project details. Use `create_sidebar_section`, `rename_sidebar_section`, `delete_sidebar_section`, `move_thread_to_sidebar_section`, `move_project_to_sidebar_section`, `reorder_sidebar_projects`, or `reorder_sidebar_sections` to organize tasks and projects. Moving an item into the pinned section pins it.

### Inline Code Comments
- Use the ::code-comment{...} directive when you need to attach feedback directly to specific code lines.
- Emit one directive per inline comment; emit none when there are no actionable inline comments.
- Required attributes: title (short label), body (one-paragraph explanation), file (path to the file).
- Optional attributes: start, end (1-based line numbers), priority (0-3).
- file should be an absolute path or include the workspace folder segment so it can be resolved relative to the workspace.
- Keep line ranges tight; end defaults to start.
- Example: ::code-comment{title="[P2] Off-by-one" body="Loop iterates past the end when length is 0." file="/path/to/foo.ts" start=10 end=11 priority=2}

### Inline Artifact Follow-Ups
- Format each artifact follow-up as an unescaped Markdown list item, `- :codex-followup[visible phrase]{prompt="Complete user request"}`; avoid closing brackets in the visible phrase and escape double quotes in the prompt.

### Git
- Branch prefix: `codex/`. Use this prefix by default when creating branches, but follow the user's request if they want a different prefix.
</app-context>

<multi_agent_role>You are `/root`, the primary agent in a team of agents collaborating to fulfill the user's goals.

At the start of your turn, you are the active agent.
You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents.
All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent without triggering a turn.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.
You can decide how much context you want to propagate to your sub-agents with the `fork_turns` parameter.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
They may be addressed as to=/root

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

*Written 2026-09-07 from observed behavior. Correct it whenever it's wrong — that is the
point of the file.*

<!-- HAND-OWNED since 2026-07-17. This file is the authority for Codex — edit it directly. On any major change, mirror it into CLAUDE.md so Claude and Codex stay in sync. -->
# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| LLM Wiki schema (source, page, ingest/query/lint rules) | `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — unprocessed only; `ingest` copies and hash-verifies them into `raw/sources/clips/`; processed originals stay until removal is authorized |
| Application code | `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `../ai-os-projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

Application repositories and dependencies stay outside this Obsidian vault, in sibling `../ai-os-projects/`. Do not create a junction from the vault back into that directory.

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**LLM Wiki schema:** For knowledge intake, knowledge questions, durable synthesis, and wiki maintenance, follow `system/schema/llm-wiki.md`. Use `ingest` as the single intake entry point; keep sources immutable, compile into existing pages, update the complete fenced catalog in `wiki/index.md`, and append changes to `wiki/log.md`. Preserve domain homes and live-system authority. Mechanical tasks retain the retrieval exceptions above.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **LSAT teaching:** use project-owned `lsat-u-voice` and `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`; this Joshua-selected standard takes precedence over older vendor voice blends.
- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft's paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **Git remote is enabled.** `origin` is GitHub (`vnjkoxpl-42905/ai-os`, decided 2026-09-29). The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.
- **New laptop restore:** GitHub contains tracked ai-os files and skills. It deliberately excludes `.env`, `raw/`, app repos, credentials, and machine sessions. Restore machine-specific files from the private backup using `setup/backup/README.md` and its `README - START HERE.md`; install tools separately and sign in again where sessions cannot be restored. Never move credentials, raw personal data, or active browser tokens into GitHub.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |
| `accommodations-email` | "accommodations email to X" (dictation: "accommodations scale"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua's explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.
- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex and Claude both activate the LLM Wiki ingest, query, and lint workflows. Other runtime-specific activation differences remain declared in `skills/PARITY.md`. Never have separate runtimes writing the same page concurrently.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

The bank lives in `public/data/PT*-S*-LR.json` and is what students see and are graded on. The DB `lr_questions` holds explanations; nothing in `src/` displays its `question_type`.\\\\n   - `normalizeQType(raw, stem)` in `src/lib/questionLoader.ts` resolves a label in this order: the canonical type, then `TYPE_SYNONYMS` (case-folded), then the label with EXCEPT or parenthetical decorations stripped, then `inferTypeFromStem`. `CANONICAL_QTYPES` holds 18 types.\\\\n   - The frozen timed catalogs are `timed_content_manifests_v1` (PT) and `timed_section_content_manifests_v1` (sections). The active hash lives in the `timed_pt_configuration_v1` and `timed_section_configuration_v1` singletons.\\\\n     - `TimedLrAdapter.content` and `verifyStandaloneLrContent` throw when the bank qtype, level or contentSha256 differs from the frozen value.\\\\n     - Generators: `npx vite-node --script scripts/knowledge/build-timed-manifest.ts` and `build-standalone-all-sections-v2.ts`. The coverage pin is in `scripts/knowledge/build-standalone-coverage.mjs` and `src/lib/timedSectionAdoption.test.ts`.\\\\n   - Seed technique: patch the old rows server-side with `jsonb_set` and verify each row with `md5(jsonb::text)` against a Python reproduction of the Postgres jsonb text format. Keys are sorted by byte length and then bytes, with \\\\\\", \\\\\\" and \\\\\\": \\\\\\" separators.\\\\n   - Render normalizer `normalizeStimulusText`: strips the shared-passage prefix and the trailing question number, joins hard wraps, and inserts speaker breaks only when the text begins with a dialogue intro.\\\\n   - EXCEPT is recorded as the `questionFeatures` \\\\\\"EXCEPT\\\\\\" tag in the bank and as a trailing \\\\\\" EXCEPT\\\\\\" in the DB `question_type`.\\\\n\\\\n3. This is ideal since I have lots of network devices that I use Claude Code to develop various software changes, build and deploy packages to for installation and smoke testing, so its imperative I actually have access to Claude Code sessions running on laptop.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nClaude Code comes to the web - so you can pay to manage the AI that\'s taking your job (https://www.windowscentral.com/artificial-intelligence/anthropics-claude-code-is-now-available-on-the-web-for-its-pro-and-max-users)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn0news32\ue201 [wordlim: 100] Published: last year; Claude clones your GitHub repository to a secure virtual machine, sets up the coding environment, and configures internet access based on your preferences. ... Claude Code on the web currently only works with GitHub repositories, and moving sessions between web and local setups requires authentication to the same account.\nEach session runs in an isolated virtual machine with tightly controlled network access, and sensitive credentials never leave the secure proxy used for authentication.\nWhen you start a task on Claude Code on the web, the process is fairly simple. Claude clones your GitHub repository to a secure virtual machine, sets up the coding environment, and configures internet access based on your preferences. From there, it analyzes your code, makes the necessary changes, runs tests, and checks its own work. Once it\'s done, you\'re notified and can review everything before creating a pull request.\nThere are still a few limitations to note. Claude Code on the web currently only works with GitHub repositories, and moving sessions between web and local setups requires authentication to the same account. It also shares rate limits with your overall Claude usage, so running several tasks in parallel will use them up faster.\nEven with these restrictions, Anthropic\'s cautious, security-first approach shows it\'s serious about making AI coding both powerful and responsible-a strong foundation for whatever comes next, whether you buy into all this stuff or not.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nClaude Code just came to the web - and it\'s about to change how you vibe code (https://www.tomsguide.com/ai/claude-code-just-came-to-the-web-and-its-about-to-change-how-you-vibe-code)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn0news33\ue201 [wordlim: 100] Published: last year; With its latest update, Claude Code on the web users can now write, fix and manage code straight from their browser. ... This updated includes GitHub integration, secure sandbox testing and support for multiple tasks at once, turning Claude Code into a full-on cloud coding workspace.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nAnthropic\'s new Claude Code web portal aims to make AI coding even more accessible (https://www.itpro.com/software/development/anthropic-claude-code-for-web-closed-beta-launch)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn0news35\ue201 [wordlim: 100] Published: last year; Claude Code for web runs entirely in a user\'s browser of choice rather than in a command-line interface (CLI) and can be connected directly to chosen GitHub repositories. ... This is part of a new sandbox runtime, released alongside Claude Code on the web, which allows admins to set out precise directories and network hosts that are accessible to the tool.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nI test AI coding tools all day - here are 9 hidden Claude Code features you\'re probably missing (https://www.tomsguide.com/ai/i-test-ai-coding-tools-all-day-here-are-9-hidden-claude-code-features-youre-probably-missing)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn0news36\ue201 [wordlim: 100] Published: 2 months ago; MCP allows you to securely connect Claude directly to outside infrastructure - like your GitHub repositories, enterprise databases, Slack channels or web search APIs. ... But "headless mode" allows Claude Code to run completely in the background without you actively sitting at your keyboard. ... You can trigger it to run automatically at midnight to audit your code for bugs, generate fresh documentation, or draft release notes for your team while you sleep. ... Stop typing out lengthy paragraphs like "Please look over this specific file, check it for syntax errors, and give me feedback."\n'}]

denied\ue201 L49:     * \ue200cite\ue20240†Errors when sending to a cloud session\ue201 L50:     * \ue200cite\ue20241†Environment expired\ue201 L51:   * \ue200cite\ue20242†Limitations\ue201 L52:   * \ue200cite\ue20243†Related resources\ue201 L53: Claude Code in the cloud\nL54: # Use Claude Code in the cloud\nL55: \nL56: Run Claude Code sessions in the cloud from your browser, phone, Desktop app, or terminal, move them with `--cloud` and `--teleport`, and auto-fix pull requests.\nL57: \nL58: Copy page Copy page\nL59: \nL60: Cloud sessions are available on Pro, Max, and Team plans, and for Enterprise users with premium seats or Chat + Claude Code seats.\nL61: A cloud session is a Claude Code session that runs on cloud infrastructure instead of on your machine. By default it runs on infrastructure Anthropic manages, or on your organization’s \ue200cite\ue20244†self-hosted environment\ue201 when routed there. The session keeps running after you close your laptop, and you can check on it or steer it from any device. To let cloud sessions clone your code from GitHub and push branches, connect GitHub with one of the \ue200cite\ue20212†GitHub connection methods\ue201 .\nL62: If your repository is on GitLab, Bitbucket, or another host, see \ue200cite\ue20242†Platform restrictions\ue201 for what works. You can start a cloud session from any of these surfaces:\nL63:   * Browser: \ue200cite\ue20245†claude.ai/code†claude.ai\ue201 , also called Claude Code on the web\nL64:   * Mobile: the Code tab in the \ue200cite\ue20246†Claude app\ue201 L65:   * Desktop app: select Cloud instead of Local when you \ue200cite\ue20247†start a session\ue201 L66:   * Terminal: \ue200cite\ue20215†`claude --cloud`\ue201 L67:   * Routines: \ue200cite\ue20248†scheduled and triggered runs\ue201 each run as a cloud session\nL68: Once you’re set up, use this page to move work between your terminal and the cloud, manage and share sessions, turn on auto-fix for pull requests, and troubleshoot.\nL69: These cases are covered on other pages:\nL70: \nL71:   * Starting your first cloud session: \ue200cite\ue20249†Get started with cloud sessions\ue201 connects GitHub and walks through a task in the browser\nL72:   * Many cloud sessions for one body of work: a \ue200cite\ue20250†project\ue201 has Claude start and keep track of them for you\nL73:   * Steering a local session from another device: sessions in your terminal, IDE, or the Desktop app with Local selected run on your machine, and \ue200cite\ue20251†Remote Control\ue201 lets you reach them from your phone or browser\nL74: ##\nL75: \nL76: \ue200cite\ue20211†\u200b\ue201 L77: \nL78: Cloud environments\nL79: \nL80: Every cloud session runs in a \ue200cite\ue20252†cloud environment\ue201 , the saved configuration that controls network access, environment variables, and setup scripts.\nL81:   * Your first environment: if you don’t have one yet, onboarding sets up a Default environment with \ue200cite\ue20253†Trusted network access\ue201 , either by creating it for you or by asking you to create it. See \ue200cite\ue20254†The Default environment\ue201 for which of those happens on your plan\nL82:   * Which environment a session uses: see \ue200cite\ue20254†The Default environment\ue201 for how sessions choose an environment when you have more than one\nL83:   * Change what sessions can reach or run at startup: see \ue200cite\ue20252†Configure cloud environments\ue201 L84:   * What’s installed without any configuration: see \ue200cite\ue20255†Installed tools\ue201 L85: ##\nL86: \nL87: \ue200cite\ue20212†\u200b\ue201 L88: \nL89: GitHub authentication options\nL90: \nL91: Cloud sessions need access to your GitHub repositories to clone code and push branches. You can grant access in two ways:\nL92: Method  | How you connect  | Repositories sessions can reach  | Best for\nL93: --- | --- | --- | ---\nL94: GitHub App  | Authorize the Claude GitHub App during \ue200cite\ue20249†web onboarding\ue201 | Any public repository, and private repositories that the Claude GitHub App is installed on  | Browser onboarding; teams that want \ue200cite\ue20233†Auto-fix\ue201 L95: `/web-setup`  | Run `/web-setup` in your terminal to send your local `gh` CLI token to your Claude account  | Any repository your `gh` token can access, whether or not the Claude GitHub App is installed  | Individual developers who already use `gh`\nL96: These features depend on the Claude GitHub App being installed on the repository:\nL97:   * Auto-fix: installing the Claude GitHub App on a repository also enables \ue200cite\ue20233†Auto-fix\ue201 for pull requests in it\nL98:   * Projects: threads in a \ue200cite\ue20250†project\ue201 need the Claude GitHub App installed on each repository they clone, whichever method you connected with. See \ue200cite\ue20256†Set up GitHub access\ue201 L99: In Anthropic-hosted environments, your GitHub credentials stay encrypted on Anthropic’s servers and never enter a session’s VM. GitHub operations from the VM go through the \ue200cite\ue20257†GitHub proxy\ue201 , which attaches the credential on the server side. See \ue200cite\ue20258†Connect from your terminal\ue201 for the `/web-setup` walkthrough, including what `/web-setup` stores and how to remove it.\nL100: Organizations with \ue200cite\ue20259†Zero Data Retention\ue201 enabled, or with the \ue200cite\ue20260†HIPAA configuration\ue201 applied, can’t use `/web-setup` or other cloud session features.\nL101: ###\nL102: \nL103: \ue200cite\ue20213†\u200b\ue201 L104: \nL105: Quick setup for Team and Enterprise\nL106: \nL107: Quick setup is an organization setting that removes steps from members’ GitHub and environment setup. On Team and Enterprise plans it’s off by default. Here’s what changes for members when it’s on:\nL108:   * `/web-setup`: members can connect GitHub with `/web-setup`. While the setting is off, the command is hidden\nL109:   * GitHub App prompt: browser onboarding skips the Claude GitHub App install prompt\nL110:   * First environment: browser onboarding creates the \ue200cite\ue20254†Default environment\ue201 for members instead of showing the environment form\nL111: An \ue200cite\ue20261†Owner\ue201 turns it on with the Quick setup toggle at \ue200cite\ue20262†Organization settings > Claude Code†claude.ai\ue201 .\nL112: ##\nL113: \nL114: \ue200cite\ue20214†\u200b\ue201 L115: \nL116: Move tasks between terminal and cloud\nL117: \nL118: These workflows require the \ue200cite\ue20263†Claude Code CLI\ue201 signed in to the same claude.ai account. You can start new cloud sessions from your terminal, or pull cloud sessions into your terminal to continue locally. Cloud sessions persist even if you close your laptop, and you can monitor them from anywhere including the Claude mobile app.\nL119: From the CLI, session handoff is one-way: you can pull cloud sessions into your terminal with `--teleport`, but you can’t push an existing terminal session to the cloud. The `--cloud` flag with a task description creates a new cloud session for your current repository; with `-p` and a session ID or claude.ai/code URL it instead \ue200cite\ue20218†queues a message into that existing session\ue201 . The \ue200cite\ue20264†Desktop app\ue201 can send a local session in its Code tab to the cloud from its Open in menu.\nL120: ###\nL121: \nL122: \ue200cite\ue20215†\u200b\ue201 L123: \nL124: From terminal to cloud\nL125: \nL126: Start a cloud session from the command line with the `--cloud` flag:\nL127: \nL128:     `claude --cloud "Fix the authentication bug in src/auth/login.ts"\nL129:     `\nL130: This creates a new cloud session on claude.ai. The cloud VM clones your current directory’s GitHub remote at your current branch, not your local checkout, so push first if you have local commits. See \ue200cite\ue20217†Send local repositories without GitHub\ue201 for the cases where Claude Code uploads your local repository instead of cloning. `--cloud` works with a single repository at a time. The task runs in the cloud while you continue working locally.\nL131: The older `--remote` spelling still works as a deprecated alias for `--cloud`. While the cloud container starts, the CLI shows a live checklist of setup steps, such as cloning the repository and running your \ue200cite\ue20265†setup script\ue201 . It queues messages you type during provisioning and sends them once the session is ready.\nL132: `--cloud` creates cloud sessions. `--remote-control` is unrelated: it lets you monitor and steer a local CLI session from claude.ai or the Claude app. See \ue200cite\ue20251†Remote Control\ue201 .\nL133: Open the session on claude.ai or the Claude mobile app to check progress or interact directly. From there you can steer Claude, provide feedback, or answer questions as in any other conversation. If Claude asks a question and the session sits idle, you can still answer when you come back, up to \ue200cite\ue20241†environment expiry\ue201 , and the session continues from your answer.\nL134: ####\nL135: \nL136: \ue200cite\ue20216†\u200b\ue201 L137: \nL138: Tips for cloud tasks\nL139: \nL140: Plan locally, execute in the cloud: for complex tasks, start Claude in plan mode to collaborate on the approach, then send work to the cloud:\nL141: \nL142:     `claude --permission-mode plan\nL143:     `\nL144: In plan mode, Claude reads files, runs commands to explore, and proposes a plan without editing source code. Once you’re satisfied, save the plan to the repo, commit, and push so the cloud VM can clone it. Then start a cloud session for autonomous execution:\nL145: \nL146:     `claude --cloud "Execute the migration plan in docs/migration-plan.md"\nL147:     `\nL148: Run tasks in parallel: each `--cloud` command creates its own cloud session that runs independently. You can start multiple tasks and they’ll all run simultaneously in separate sessions:\nL149: \nL150:     `claude --cloud "Fix the flaky test in auth.spec.ts"\nL151:     claude --cloud "Update the API documentation"\nL152:     claude --cloud "Refactor the logger to use structured output"\nL153:     `\nL154: \nL155: When a session completes, you can create a PR from claude.ai/code or \ue200cite\ue20220†teleport\ue201 the session to your terminal to continue working.\nL156: ####\nL157: \nL158: \ue200cite\ue20217†\u200b\ue201 L159: \nL160: Send local repositories without GitHub\nL161: When you run `claude --cloud` from a repository that has no git remote, or from a github.com repository that the Claude GitHub App isn’t installed on, Claude Code bundles your local repository and uploads it directly to the cloud session. This applies even if you connected GitHub with `/web-setup`. For a full clone, the bundle includes your repository history across all branches, plus uncommitted changes to tracked files. What happens to uncommitted changes in sensitive files depends on your platform:\nL162:   * macOS, Linux, and WSL: Claude Code leaves uncommitted changes to files named like credentials or keys out of the upload. This includes `.env` files, Terraform `*.tfvars` files, and key files such as `id_rsa` and `*.pem`. It also leaves out uncommitted changes to files that a git filter such as Git LFS manages. A `Left on this machine:` notice names the files left out, and the session starts with the committed version of each, or without the file if none is committed.\nL163:   * Native Windows: uncommitted changes to tracked files upload as they are, whatever the file’s name. Stash or revert an edit you don’t want in the cloud session before you start it.\nL164: To upload a bundle even when Claude Code would otherwise clone from the remote, set `CCR_FORCE_BUNDLE=1`:\nL165: \nL166:     `CCR_FORCE_BUNDLE=1 claude --cloud "Run the test suite and fix any failures"\nL167:     `\nL168: Bundled repositories must meet these limits:\nL169:   * The directory must be a git repository with at least one commit\nL170:   * The bundled repository must be under 100 MB. Larger repositories fall back to bundling only the current branch, then to a single squashed snapshot of the working tree, and fail if the snapshot is still too large\nL171:   * Untracked files are not included; run `git add` on files you want the cloud session to see\nL172:   * On macOS, Linux, and WSL, Claude Code refuses the upload when it can’t follow a git setting that affects which attribute rules apply to your files, such as `core.attributesFile` set in an included config file. The \ue200cite\ue20266†refusal message\ue201 names the setting and the fix\nL173:   * Sessions created from a bundle can push back to a GitHub remote only when your \ue200cite\ue20212†GitHub connection\ue201 has push access to that repository\nL174: On macOS, Linux, and WSL, the upload also needs git 2.31 or later and a checkout layout it supports, while on native Windows Claude Code uploads without either check. When a checkout doesn’t meet those requirements, Claude Code doesn’t start the session. It prints an error that contains `Not uploading this working tree:`, names the cause, and says what to change. These are the common causes:\nL175:   * Older git: the installed git is older than 2.31. Update git, then retry.\nL176:   * A checkout layout the upload doesn’t support: you started inside a submodule, in a clone made with `git clone --separate-git-dir`, `--shared`, or `--reference`, in a checkout with `core.worktree` set, or in a repository that keeps its refs in the reftable format. Start from the main checkout of a clone made with a plain `git clone` instead.\nL177:   * A linked worktree with a sparse checkout: `git sparse-checkout` writes settings to the worktree’s own `config.worktree` file, which the upload doesn’t accept, so a worktree that has those settings isn’t uploaded, and neither is one Claude Code created with \ue200cite\ue20267†`worktree.sparsePaths`\ue201 . Start from the repository’s main checkout instead.\nL178:   * Git configuration kept inside the working tree: your git configuration includes a file that sits inside the checkout, for example an `include.path` entry that points into the repository. Move that file outside the working tree or remove the include, then retry.\nL179: On macOS, Linux, and WSL, a partial clone made with `git clone --filter` uploads as a snapshot of its working tree without history, as long as the clone holds every tracked file locally. For `claude --cloud`, if the repository is on GitHub, you can avoid the upload and its requirements: push your branch, install the Claude GitHub App on the repository, and start the session again so that it clones from GitHub.\nL180: ###\nL181: \nL182: \ue200cite\ue20218†\u200b\ue201 L183: \nL184: Send follow-ups from the CLI\nL185: \nL186: Once a cloud session is running, wherever it executes, send it a follow-up message from the `claude` CLI on any machine where you’re logged in with `claude auth login`. The CLI authenticates with your Anthropic account credentials and sends no local session state, so the command doesn’t need to run from the machine that started the session. The command posts one message and exits:\nL187: \nL188:     `claude -p "your message" --cloud <session-i d>\nL189:     `\nL190: The CLI queues the message into the session and exits without waiting for a reply. Use it to steer a long-running session, queue the next step while the current one is still finishing, or send follow-ups from a \ue200cite\ue20268†CI script\ue201 . You can also pipe the message on stdin instead of passing it as an argument: `echo "your message" | claude -p --cloud <session-id>`.\nL191: For `<session-id>`, pass the bare ID, such as `session_...` or `cse_...`, or the session’s `claude.ai/code/<id>` URL, with or without the scheme or query string. Find the ID in your session list at claude.ai/code.\nL192: `--cloud` requires an Anthropic account. It’s not available when Claude Code is configured for Amazon Bedrock, Google Cloud’s Agent Platform, or another third-party provider. An \ue200cite\ue20269†LLM gateway\ue201 configured only through `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` doesn’t count as a third-party provider for this check, but you still need to sign in with `claude auth login`. Your organization’s `allow_remote_sessions` policy must also be enabled.\nL193: An Owner can turn it on in the Claude Code admin settings at claude.ai/admin-settings/claude-code.\nL194: ####\nL195: \nL196: \ue200cite\ue20219†\u200b\ue201 L197: \nL198: Output\nL199: \nL200: On success, the command prints the session ID and a link to view the session:\nL201: \nL202:     `Sent to cloud session.\nL203:     Session ID: session_01DiUkqY2kzbUbDmW1w96rfi\nL204:     View: https://claude.ai/code/session_01DiUkqY2kzbUbDmW1w96rfi?from=cli&m=0\nL205:     `\nL206: Pass `--output-format json` for a machine-readable result: `{ok, session_id, url}` on success, or `{ok: false, session_id, error}` when the send fails, for example when the session is missing or archived. Configuration errors, such as an unsupported provider or a disabled organization policy, print to stderr without JSON. `--output-format stream-json` isn’t supported with `--cloud <session-id>`. If the send fails, see \ue200cite\ue20240†Errors when sending to a cloud session\ue201 .\nL207: ###\nL208: \nL209: \ue200cite\ue20220†\u200b\ue201 L210: \nL211: From cloud to terminal\nL212: \nL213: Pull a cloud session into your terminal using any of these:\nL214:   * Using `--teleport`: from the command line, run `claude --teleport` for an interactive session picker, or `claude --teleport <session-id>` to resume a specific session directly. If you have uncommitted changes, you’ll be prompted to stash them first.\nL215:   * Using `/teleport`: inside an existing CLI session, run `/teleport` or `/tp` to open the same session picker without restarting Claude Code.\nL216:   * From `/tasks`: run `/tasks` to see your background sessions, then press `t` to teleport into one.\nL217:   * From claude.ai/c…24 tokens truncated…terminal.\nL218:   * From inside the cloud session: type `/teleport` and Claude Code replies with the exact `claude --teleport <session-id>` command for that session, ready to run from a checkout of the repository. Requires Claude Code v2.1.223 or later in the session’s environment.\nL219: When you teleport a session, Claude verifies you’re in the correct repository, fetches and checks out the branch from the cloud session, and loads the full conversation history into your terminal. The terminal gets its own copy of the session: new work there stays local and doesn’t appear in the cloud session on claude.ai or the Claude mobile app. To keep steering from your phone after teleporting, start \ue200cite\ue20251†`/remote-control`\ue201 in the local session. `--teleport` is distinct from `--resume`.\nL220: `--resume` reopens a conversation from this machine’s local history and doesn’t list cloud sessions; `--teleport` pulls a cloud session and its branch.\nL221: ####\nL222: \nL223: \ue200cite\ue20221†\u200b\ue201 L224: \nL225: Teleport requirements\nL226: \nL227: Teleport checks these requirements before resuming a session. If any requirement isn’t met, you’ll see an error or be prompted to resolve the issue.\nL228: Requirement  | Details\nL229: --- | ---\nL230: Clean git state  | Your working directory must have no uncommitted changes. Teleport prompts you to stash changes if needed.\nL231: Correct repository  | You must run `--teleport` from a checkout of the same repository, not a fork. If you run it from a checkout of a different repository, Claude Code shows an error that names both the session’s repository and your checkout’s. If Claude Code can’t parse your remote into a hostname, for example an SSH host alias like `git@work:owner/repo.git`, it asks you to confirm, and accepts the checkout when the remote’s owner and repository name match the session’s repository.\nL232: Branch available  | The branch from the cloud session must have been pushed to the remote. Teleport automatically fetches and checks it out.\nL233: Same account  | You must be authenticated to the same claude.ai account used in the cloud session.\nL234: When teleport fetches the session’s branch, the fetch never waits for input in your terminal. If git or ssh would ask for a password, a key passphrase, or confirmation of a new SSH host, the fetch fails, and the checkout then works only if your local clone already has the branch. For the two SSH cases, load your key into `ssh-agent` and run `git fetch` once by hand first to record the host.\nL235: ####\nL236: \nL237: \ue200cite\ue20222†\u200b\ue201 L238: \nL239: `--teleport` is unavailable\nL240: \nL241: Teleport requires claude.ai subscription authentication. Find the case that matches yours:\nL242: \nL243:   * You’re authenticated via API key: run `/login` to sign in with your claude.ai account instead\nL244:   * The error names your provider: cloud sessions aren’t available through third-party providers. See the \ue200cite\ue20240†error table\ue201 L245:   * You’re already signed in via claude.ai: your organization may have disabled cloud sessions\nL246: ##\nL247: \nL248: \ue200cite\ue20223†\u200b\ue201 L249: \nL250: Work with sessions\nL251: \nL252: Sessions appear in the sidebar at claude.ai/code. From there you can review changes, share with teammates, archive finished work, or delete sessions permanently.\nL253: ###\nL254: \nL255: \ue200cite\ue20224†\u200b\ue201 L256: \nL257: Permission modes in cloud sessions\nL258: \nL259: You pick a cloud session’s \ue200cite\ue20270†permission mode\ue201 from the \ue200cite\ue20271†mode dropdown\ue201 , both when you create the task and while the session runs. Claude Code resumes a session in the permission mode it was in when you do either of these:\nL260: \nL261:   * Reopen a session whose Anthropic-hosted \ue200cite\ue20241†environment expired\ue201 L262:   * Send a message to a session that a self-hosted runner \ue200cite\ue20272†released while it was idle\ue201 L263: ###\nL264: \nL265: \ue200cite\ue20225†\u200b\ue201 L266: \nL267: Review changes\nL268: Each session shows a diff indicator with lines added and removed, like `+42 -18`. Select it to open the diff view, leave inline comments on specific lines, and send them to Claude with your next message. The diff view compares the session’s changes against its base branch by default. To compare against any other branch in the repository, select Compare against and pick one.\nL269: Claude Code computes these diffs from raw git blob content, so diff drivers and `textconv` filters configured in the repository don’t apply. These steps are covered elsewhere:\nL270:   * The full walkthrough, including PR creation: see \ue200cite\ue20273†Review and iterate\ue201 L271:   * Having Claude monitor the PR for CI failures and review comments automatically: see \ue200cite\ue20233†Auto-fix pull requests\ue201 L272: ###\nL273: \nL274: \ue200cite\ue20226†\u200b\ue201 L275: \nL276: Manage context\nL277: \nL278: Cloud sessions support \ue200cite\ue20274†built-in commands\ue201 that produce text output. Commands that only run in the terminal interface, such as `/plugin` or `/resume`, aren’t available. Commands that open a picker or panel in the terminal behave differently in cloud sessions:\nL279:   * `/model`, `/effort`, `/color`, and `/rename`: pass the value as an argument, for example `/model sonnet`, instead of opening the terminal picker or slider. The argument forms require Claude Code v2.1.205 or later in the session’s environment and follow each command’s \ue200cite\ue20275†availability notes\ue201 .\nL280:   * `/fast`: toggles \ue200cite\ue20276†fast mode\ue201 for the session when fast mode is \ue200cite\ue20277†available on your account\ue201 . Requires Claude Code v2.1.271 or later in the session’s environment.\nL281:   * `/config`: in your browser at claude.ai/code, opens the Claude Code section of your settings instead of setting a value, and text after the command, including `key=value`, is ignored. To change a setting for a cloud session, set an \ue200cite\ue20278†environment variable\ue201 on the environment, or in a session with one repository, commit the key to that repository’s `.claude/settings.json`. \ue200cite\ue20279†Settings in cloud sessions\ue201 lists what each session reads.\nL282: For context management specifically:\nL283: Command  | Works in cloud sessions  | Notes\nL284: --- | --- | ---\nL285: `/compact`  | Yes  | Summarizes the conversation to free up context. Accepts optional focus instructions like `/compact keep the test output`\nL286: `/context`  | Yes  | Shows what’s currently in the context window\nL287: `/clear`  | No  | Start a new session from the sidebar instead\nL288: Auto-compaction runs automatically when the context window approaches capacity. Cloud sessions set \ue200cite\ue20280†`CLAUDE_AUTOCOMPACT_PCT_OVERRIDE`\ue201 themselves, so compaction triggers partway through the \ue200cite\ue20281†auto-compact window\ue201 rather than when the window fills. That value overrides one you add in your \ue200cite\ue20278†environment variables\ue201 , so adding the variable there doesn’t change when compaction triggers.\nL289: To change the auto-compact window instead, set \ue200cite\ue20280†`CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW`\ue201 in your environment variables, or run \ue200cite\ue20275†`/autocompact`\ue201 with a token count in a session where the variable isn’t set. \ue200cite\ue20282†Subagents\ue201 work the same way they do locally. Claude can spawn them with the Agent tool to offload research or parallel work into a separate context window, keeping the main conversation lighter. Subagents defined in your repo’s `.claude/agents/` are picked up automatically.\nL290: \ue200cite\ue20283†Agent teams\ue201 are off by default but can be enabled by adding `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` to your \ue200cite\ue20278†environment variables\ue201 .\nL291: ###\nL292: \nL293: \ue200cite\ue20227†\u200b\ue201 L294: \nL295: Take back a queued message\nL296: \nL297: If you send a message while Claude is working, the message queues until Claude reads it. To take a queued message back, click the ✕ on it. The text returns to the message box so you can edit it or send something else. If Claude has already read the message, it stays in the conversation.\nL298: ###\nL299: \nL300: \ue200cite\ue20228†\u200b\ue201 L301: \nL302: Share sessions\nL303: \nL304: To share a session, toggle its visibility according to the account types below. After that, share the session link as-is. Recipients see the latest state when they open the link, but their view doesn’t update in real time.\nL305: ####\nL306: \nL307: \ue200cite\ue20229†\u200b\ue201 L308: \nL309: Share from an Enterprise or Team account\nL310: \nL311: Sharing works as follows for Enterprise and Team accounts:\nL312:   * Visibility options: Private and Team. Team visibility makes the session visible to other members of your claude.ai organization\nL313:   * Repository access: verification is enabled by default, based on the GitHub account connected to the recipient’s account\nL314:   * Your name: your account’s display name is visible to all recipients with access\nL315:   * Slack sessions: \ue200cite\ue20284†Claude in Slack\ue201 sessions are automatically shared with Team visibility\nL316: ####\nL317: \nL318: \ue200cite\ue20230†\u200b\ue201 L319: \nL320: Share from a Max or Pro account\nL321: \nL322: Sharing works as follows for Max and Pro accounts:\nL323:   * Visibility options: Private and Public. Public visibility makes the session visible to any user logged into claude.ai\nL324:   * Repository access: verification isn’t enabled by default\nL325:   * Sensitive content: check your session before sharing. Sessions may contain code and credentials from private GitHub repositories\nL326: To require recipients to have repository access, or to hide your name from shared sessions, go to \ue200cite\ue20285†Settings > Claude Code > Sharing settings†claude.ai\ue201 .\nL327: ###\nL328: \nL329: \ue200cite\ue20231†\u200b\ue201 L330: \nL331: Archive sessions\nL332: \nL333: You can archive sessions to keep your session list organized. Archived sessions are hidden from the default session list but can be viewed by filtering for archived sessions. To archive a session, hover over the session in the sidebar and select the archive icon.\nL334: ###\nL335: \nL336: \ue200cite\ue20232†\u200b\ue201 L337: \nL338: Delete sessions\nL339: \nL340: Deleting a session permanently removes the session and its data. This action can’t be undone. You can delete a session in two ways:\nL341: \nL342:   * From the sidebar: filter for archived sessions, then hover over the session you want to delete and select the delete icon\nL343:   * From the session menu: open a session, select the dropdown next to the session title, and select Delete\nL344: You will be asked to confirm before a session is deleted.\nL345: ##\nL346: \nL347: \ue200cite\ue20233†\u200b\ue201 L348: \nL349: Auto-fix pull requests\nL350: \nL351: Claude can watch a pull request and automatically respond to CI failures and review comments. Claude subscribes to GitHub activity on the PR, and when a check fails or a reviewer leaves a comment, Claude investigates and pushes a fix if one is clear.\nL352: \nL353: Auto-fix requires the Claude GitHub App to be installed on your repository. If you haven’t already, install it from the \ue200cite\ue20286†GitHub App page†github.com\ue201 .\nL354: There are a few ways to turn on auto-fix depending on where the PR came from and what device you’re using:\nL355:   * PRs created in a cloud session: open the session at claude.ai/code, open the CI status bar, and select Auto-fix\nL356:   * From your terminal: run \ue200cite\ue20274†`/autofix-pr`\ue201 while on the PR’s branch. Claude Code detects the open PR with `gh`, spawns a cloud session, and turns on auto-fix in one step\nL357:   * From the mobile app: tell Claude to auto-fix the PR, for example “watch this PR and fix any CI failures or review comments”\nL358:   * Any existing PR: paste the PR URL into a session and tell Claude to auto-fix it\nL359: Auto-fix is a per-PR toggle. To stop monitoring, open the CI status bar in the session at claude.ai/code and clear the Auto-fix toggle, or tell Claude to stop watching the PR.\nL360: ###\nL361: \nL362: \ue200cite\ue20234†\u200b\ue201 L363: \nL364: How Claude responds to PR activity\nL365: \nL366: When auto-fix is active, Claude receives GitHub events for the PR including new review comments and CI check failures. For each event, Claude investigates and decides how to proceed:\nL367:   * Clear fixes: if Claude is confident in a fix and it doesn’t conflict with earlier instructions, Claude makes the change, pushes it, and explains what was done in the session\nL368:   * Ambiguous requests: if a reviewer’s comment could be interpreted multiple ways or involves something architecturally significant, Claude asks you before acting\nL369:   * Duplicate or no-action events: if an event is a duplicate or requires no change, Claude notes it in the session and moves on\nL370: GitHub does not emit a webhook when the base branch advances and creates a merge conflict, so auto-fix can’t react to conflicts on its own. To resolve a conflict, open the session and ask Claude to rebase. Claude may reply to review comment threads on GitHub as part of resolving them. These replies are posted using your GitHub account, so they appear under your username, but each reply is labeled as coming from Claude Code so reviewers know it was written by the agent and not by you directly.\nL371: If your repository uses comment-triggered automation such as Atlantis, Terraform Cloud, or custom GitHub Actions that run on `issue_comment` events, be aware that Claude can reply on your behalf, which can trigger those workflows. Review your repository’s automation before enabling auto-fix, and consider disabling auto-fix for repositories where a PR comment can deploy infrastructure or run privileged operations.\nL372: ##\nL373: \nL374: \ue200cite\ue20235†\u200b\ue201 L375: \nL376: Security and isolation\nL377: \nL378: Each cloud session is separated from your machine and from other sessions through several layers:\nL379:   * Isolated virtual machines: each session runs in an isolated, Anthropic-managed VM. Sessions your organization routes to a \ue200cite\ue20244†self-hosted environment\ue201 run on your own infrastructure instead, where isolation is your deployment’s responsibility\n\n{"chunk_id":"b004b5","wall_time_seconds":0.2725352,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1421,"output":"wiki/log.md:157:- Second pass, same day: signed-in DOM check of the theatre in his Chrome (Round 8 src and poster, 3 chapters, 9 tab stops); decode check of all three files in the in-app browser (1920x1080, plays continuously); frames from the shipped bytes at 17.5 / 30 / 62 / 101.5 / 128 s.\\r\\nwiki/log.md:388:**The backup had been silently failing for three nights.** Commit `395fafc` (\\"Slim system/scripts: remove 15 one-shot migration/rename scripts\\", 2026-07-17 22:38) deleted `watchdog.sh` and `cas-replace.sh` on the stated grounds that they were \\"unreferenced by ... the backup job chain.\\" That was wrong: `with-lock.sh:13` execs `watchdog.sh` on every run, and `run-job.sh` routes every job through `with-lock.sh`. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.1 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"5db1af","wall_time_seconds":0.2431469,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1984,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text':
